description: "The ZX Spectrum Next's picture from above: the five producers of dots that run side by side (the ULA, LoRes, Layer 2, the tilemap and the sprites) and the four layers they feed, the areas each can draw in, the two answers every layer gives for every dot, the pipeline that turns those answers into one 9-bit colour, the clocks it runs on, and how Klive's emulator composes the same picture."

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# The Big Picture: Five Producers, Four Layers, One Dot

Load a modern game on a ZX Spectrum Next and look at one frame. A playfield built from tiles
scrolls smoothly from right to left. Behind it a background in a few hundred colours scrolls more
slowly, for depth. Dozens of characters move over both. At the bottom sits a score panel in the
unmistakable Spectrum font and colours. And the same machine, switched to another mode, runs a 1982
48K game exactly as the 48K did, down to the T-state. How can one screen be all of that at once?

On the original Spectrum, one chip produced every dot. On the Next, **five producers of dots** run
side by side, each reading its own memory. They feed **four layers**: two of the producers, the ULA
and LoRes, share one layer and take turns at it. So for every position on the screen there are four
candidate dots, and something has to pick one of them, about fourteen million times a second. No
pressure.

Why should you care how that choice is made? Because nearly every surprise a Next program gives you on
screen is that choice at work. A sprite that vanishes behind the background, a score panel that shows
through a hole in Layer 2, a magenta that turns out to be no colour at all: each one is a rule of the
machinery you are about to meet. Know the map, and you know where to look when the picture is not the
one you meant to draw.

This is the first of the screen chapters. The ones that follow take each piece apart; here you see how
the pieces fit. You should know the Spectrum's own screen first:
[ZX Spectrum: How the Screen Is Drawn](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/spectrum-display/)
explains the ULA, the bitmap and its attributes, and contention, and the screen chapters re-explain only
what the Next changes. [How a Television Draws a Picture](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/crt-display/)
explains the beam and the lines, and
[The ZX Spectrum Next Core and the FPGA](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-core/) explains
why the Next's hardware lives in an FPGA, and how Klive's emulator is checked against it.

## The road ahead

We start with what the Next kept from the Spectrum and what it built beside it, then meet the cast:
the five producers, the four layers they make, the border and the fallback colour. Next comes the
question of where each layer may draw, with the display area, the wide area, the clip windows that
fence a layer in and the scroll offsets that move its picture without moving a byte. Then the one idea
that carries all the screen chapters: every layer gives exactly two answers for every dot. If you read
only one section, make it that one. After that we follow those answers through the video pipeline,
stage by stage, and one dot all the way to the screen. We count the clocks the picture runs on and the
512 colours it ends as, look at what stays exactly as it was on the Spectrum, walk through the
chapters that follow, and finish with how Klive composes the same picture.

<Callout type="info">
The numbers in the screen chapters are for the Next's **128K/+3 display timing at 50 Hz**, with the
master clock at exactly 28 MHz (VGA mode 0, or HDMI). The other timings change the size of the frame,
not the way it is drawn; [the frame chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/timing/)
lists them. Every fact was checked against the Next's hardware design, and Klive emulates the machine
the same way.
</Callout>

## From one chip to a pipeline

The ZX Spectrum's picture has a single source. The ULA reads the screen memory, turns bytes into
dots and sends them to the television; nothing else in the machine takes part. The Next keeps that
ULA, and keeps it compatible to the bit: the same bitmap, the same attributes, the same order of
reads, the same contention. Every 48K or 128K program that writes to `$4000` sees exactly the screen
it expects.

What the Next adds is not a better ULA but **more producers beside it**. Each is a separate piece of
hardware with its own memory and its own idea of what a picture is: a bitmap with a byte per dot, a
grid of tiles, a set of sprites. They all run at the same time, in step with the same beam. For each
position the beam reaches, they offer **four candidate dots**, one for each layer: the ULA and LoRes
share a layer, and only one of them fills it (the cast, below, says why). A **compositor** after them
picks the winner.

<figure>

Bottom, the ZX Spectrum Next: banks 5 and 7 in the FPGA's block RAM feed the ULA, LoRes and the tilemap; the external SRAM feeds Layer 2; a 16K pattern memory, fast block RAM read at 28 MHz, feeds the sprites. The ULA and LoRes join into one input, either one or the other; with the tilemap, Layer 2 and the sprites, four inputs feed one compositor, which outputs one dot." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>One path against five producers, but only four layers: the ULA and LoRes share one input to the compositor, and only one of them fills it. LoRes is drawn dashed because, when it is on, it takes the ULA's place.</figcaption>
</figure>

Look at the memories on the left. The producers do not queue for one memory, as the CPU and the
ULA do on the Spectrum. The FPGA keeps **bank 5** (and the first 8K of bank 7, the 128K's second
screen) in its own block RAM, which has two ports: one is shared by the CPU and LoRes, the other by
the ULA and the tilemap. **Layer 2** reads the external SRAM, the Next's main memory, where its
bitmap can be large. The **sprites** keep their shapes in a 16K memory of their own, which the CPU
can reach only through I/O ports. It is block RAM inside the FPGA, and a very fast one: the sprite
hardware can read a new byte from it at **every tick of the 28 MHz master clock**, four times for
every dot the beam draws. That speed is what lets it draw dozens of sprites into a line in the time
the beam takes to draw one (the sprites chapter's
[time budget](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/sprites/#the-time-budget-of-a-line) shows
how). Giving each producer its own way to its data is what lets them all run at once.

## The cast of layers

Here are the five producers and the two colours that are not producers, each with what makes it
different. Five producers make **four layers**, because the ULA and LoRes share one. Each has a chapter
of its own later; this is the introduction, so you know who is who when the action starts.

A ray from the eye at the top goes straight down through them at one screen position: it passes a transparent point on the sprite plane and on the Layer 2 plane, and stops at an opaque tile on the tilemap plane, which wins. The ULA plane below is not reached. On the right, the composed picture shows the same scene flattened, with the winning dot marked." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="630" y="302" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">the picture</text>
</svg>
<figcaption>The layers pulled apart, in their default order. For every position of the beam the compositor looks down through the planes and takes the first dot that is not transparent; here it is a tile, and the ULA below it is never seen at that spot.</figcaption>
</figure>

The layers are a **stack**. At every dot, the top layer is shown if it has something there. Where it
is **transparent**, the dot comes from the layer below it; where that one is transparent too, from
the next one down, and so on to the bottom of the stack. Only where every layer is transparent does
the fallback colour show. Two answers for every dot, further on, explains what makes a layer
transparent at a dot.

The order of the stack is **not fixed**. The Sprite and Layers System register, NextReg `$15`, picks
one of six orders for the sprites, Layer 2 and the ULA (with the tilemap), so a program can put Layer 2
in front of the sprites, or the Spectrum screen in front of everything. The tilemap can sit in front of
the ULA or behind it, for the whole map or tile by tile. The figures here use the default order, the one
the machine starts with: the sprites on top, then Layer 2, then the tilemap, then the ULA. The decision
stage of the pipeline is where the order is applied, and the composition chapter's
[six orders](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/composition/#six-orders) shows
all of them.

- **The ULA.** The Spectrum's own screen, `$4000` in bank 5 or the shadow screen in bank 7, with
  all of its rules. The Next lets it do more when a program asks: the Timex modes (a colour for
  every 8 × 1 dots, or 512 dots across), **ULANext** and **ULA+** (more colours from the same
  attribute byte), hardware scrolling and a clip window. Old software never asks, so it never
  notices.
- **LoRes.** A 128 × 96 screen with a byte, any of 256 colours, for every 2 × 2 dots. It is not a
  layer of its own: when it is switched on, it **replaces the ULA's picture** and takes the ULA's
  place in everything that follows, its priority, its clip window, its transparency. The two are
  never shown together: the ULA layer shows the ULA's screen or LoRes, and only the border around it
  stays the ULA's either way.
- **Layer 2.** A plain bitmap with **one byte for every dot**, in 256 × 192, 320 × 256 or
  640 × 256 (half a byte a dot). No attributes, no clash, any dot any colour, at the price of memory:
  48K or 80K, which is why it lives in 16K banks of the main RAM.
- **The tilemap.** A grid of 8 × 8 **tiles**, 40 or 80 across and 32 down, each tile a reference
  into a set of tile shapes and a byte saying how to draw it: mirrored, rotated, with which colours,
  in front of the ULA or behind it. A whole screen costs a few kilobytes.
- **The sprites.** **128 sprites** of 16 × 16 dots, each with its own position, shape, colours,
  mirroring, rotation and size, which can be linked into bigger objects. The hardware never holds a
  picture of them: it draws each line of them just before the beam needs it.
- **The border.** On the Next, the border is not a separate thing: it is **what the ULA layer
  shows outside its 256 × 192 area**, in the colour of port `$FE`. It takes part in the composition
  like any other ULA dot, which is why sprites and Layer 2 can be drawn over it.
- **The fallback colour.** When every layer says "nothing here" for a dot, the picture still needs
  a colour. The Fallback Colour register, NextReg `$4A`, holds it.

Here is one scene, the screen of a game in progress, split into what each layer contributes:

## Where each layer can draw

The Spectrum has one rectangle that matters, the 256 × 192 display area, and a border around it.
The Next adds a second rectangle: the **wide area**, 320 × 256 dots, which is the 256 × 192 area
with 32 dots added on every side. The wide area covers most of the border a television shows, and it
is the canvas of the layers the Next added.

Outside the wide area only the border is shown." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>The 128K/+3 frame at 50 Hz, to scale: a 7 MHz dot across is as long as a line down. The grey parts are never seen. The dashed rectangle is the wide area; the border fills the visible frame around it.</figcaption>
</figure>

Which layer can draw where follows from what each was built to show:

| Layer | Its canvas | Can it cover the border? |
|---|---|---|
| ULA (and the border) | the whole visible frame: its 256 × 192 picture, and the border colour everywhere else | it *is* the border |
| LoRes | 256 × 192 (its 128 × 96 dots doubled) | no |
| Layer 2 | 256 × 192, or 320 × 256 and 640 × 256 in the wide area | in the wide modes, up to the wide area's edge |
| Tilemap | 320 × 256, always the wide area | up to the wide area's edge |
| Sprites | 320 × 256 coordinate space | only when "sprites over border" is on (NextReg `$15` bit 1); otherwise clipped to 256 × 192 |

So beyond the wide area, at the far edges of the screen, only the border colour is ever shown. A
monitor on HDMI shows a window of **360 × 288** dots of this frame: the wide area with 16 dots of
border to its left, 24 to its right and 16 lines above and below. Klive's emulator shows the same
window.

## Clip windows: a rectangle for each layer

A layer does not have to cover the whole of its canvas. Each layer has a **clip window**, a
rectangle on the screen. Inside it, the layer draws as usual. Outside it, the layer answers
"transparent" for every dot, so the dot comes from the layers below it, exactly as if the layer had
nothing to show there (Two answers for every dot, further on, explains the answer).

Notice what a clip window is *not*. It does not change the layer's memory, and it does not move or
shrink the layer's picture: it is a mask laid over the screen, which hides part of the picture and
shows what is underneath. That makes it the cheapest tool on the machine for a whole family of
effects: a score panel that one layer must not draw over, a playfield framed by a border, a layer
switched off for a band of the screen, a letterbox. One write of four bytes does it, where clearing
the same area in memory would take thousands of bytes, and putting it back would take thousands
more. I would call that a bargain.

There are four windows, one for each layer (LoRes, which takes the ULA's place, uses the ULA's):

| Window | NextReg | Coordinates | One X unit | After reset |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ULA and LoRes | NextReg `$1A` | the display area | 1 dot | 0–255, 0–191 |
| Layer 2 | NextReg `$18` | the display area in 256 × 192; the wide area in 320 × 256 and 640 × 256 | 1 dot; 2 dots in the wide modes | 0–255, 0–191 |
| Tilemap | NextReg `$1B` | the wide area | 2 dots | 0–159, 0–255 |
| Sprites | NextReg `$19` | the display area; with "over border" (NextReg `$15` bit 1), the whole wide area, or the window in wide-area units if NextReg `$15` bit 5 is set | 1 dot; 2 dots over the border | 0–255, 0–191 |

Each window is four bytes, **X1, X2, Y1, Y2**, and both edges are inside it: a window of X 0–255 is
256 dots wide. Your program writes all four to the same NextReg, one after the other; a small index
inside the machine says which coordinate the next write sets, steps on after every write, and wraps
from Y2 back to X1. The Clip Window Control register, NextReg `$1C`, resets the index of any of the
four windows, so you can start from X1 without counting.

<svg viewBox="0 0 700 386" role="img" aria-label="The two coordinate systems of the clip windows, side by side.   <text x="700" y="64" textAnchor="end" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5" fillOpacity="0.8">159</text>
  <text x="386" y="82" textAnchor="end" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5" fillOpacity="0.8">0</text>

  <text x="545" y="340" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">the wide area:</text>
  <text x="545" y="359" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">1 unit = 2 dots across, 1 line down</text>
  <text x="545" y="378" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5" fillOpacity="0.75">X 0–159, Y 0–255</text>

<figcaption>Two coordinate systems for the same four bytes, drawn at the same scale. A clip window is always in screen coordinates: it does not move when the layer's picture scrolls.</figcaption>
</figure>

The right half of the figure explains the odd numbers. A coordinate is one byte, 0 to 255, but the
wide area is 320 dots across. So the windows that work in the wide area count X in **units of two
dots**: X1 = 10 means dot 20, and X2 = 159 means dots up to 319, the right edge. The Y coordinates
stay in lines, and 0–255 covers the wide area's 256 lines exactly. One thing follows from that, and
let me warn you, it surprises programmers: Layer 2's window starts as 0–255, 0–191 in every mode, which
in the 320 × 256 mode covers the full width but only the top **192 of the 256 lines**. If your program
uses the wide modes, set the window to 0–159, 0–255 first.

Here is a window on the machine itself. The scene from the cast, once more, with Layer 2's window
narrowed:

The panel, the tiles and the sprites are unchanged." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

The ULA's window is special in one way: the border is never clipped. A window that leaves only the
top sixteen lines of the ULA's picture, as in the scene, still leaves the whole border.

## Scrolling without moving memory

Moving a picture on the Spectrum meant moving bytes. To scroll the screen one character to the left,
a program had to copy almost all of the 6,912 bytes of the screen.

Scrolling by **one dot** is harder still. Eight dots share a byte, so no byte can simply be copied:
every bitmap byte has to be shifted by one bit, and the bit that falls out of it carried into its
neighbour, along every line, in the screen's scrambled address order. Even with the tightest code,
an unrolled `RL (HL)` and `DEC L` for each byte, that is 19 T-states for each of the 6,144 bytes:
about 117,000 T-states, more than one and a half frames of a 48K, before the contention of the screen
memory slows it down further. And that moves only the dots. The colours belong to 8 × 8 cells and
can move only a whole character at a time, so the picture tears into ink that has moved and colours
that have not. That is why Spectrum games that scroll smoothly scroll a small window, not the whole
screen.

Bigger pictures make it worse: to move a 48K Layer 2 picture by copying, a 3.5 MHz CPU would need
about fourteen frames. All of this is far too slow for anything that has to move every frame.

So the Next lets the hardware do it. All the producers except the sprites have **scroll offsets**:
registers that say where in its picture the layer starts reading. With an offset of zero, the dot at
the top-left of the layer's area is the first dot of its picture. With an X offset of 96, it is the
97th dot of the first line, and everything shifts 96 dots to the left. One register write moves the
whole picture.

And the picture **wraps round**. What is pushed off the left edge does not disappear; it comes back
in on the right, and what leaves the top comes back at the bottom. The picture behaves like a loop,
or like the surface of a doughnut:

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 296" role="img" aria-label="How a scroll offset wraps. Left: the layer's picture in memory, divided into four pieces by the scroll offset, X 96 and Y 64: A is the part right of and below the offset, B the part left of it and below, C the part right of it and above, D the part left of it and above. Right: what the screen shows: A in the top left, B to its right, C below A and D below B. The picture is shown starting at the offset, and what runs off its right or bottom edge continues from its left or top edge." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

</svg>
<figcaption>Scrolling moves where the layer starts reading its picture, not where it draws. The red dot, the scroll offset, becomes the screen's top-left corner, and the picture wraps round: the pieces A to D swap places, and no byte of memory moves.</figcaption>
</figure>

Wrapping is what makes endless scrolling work. A game that scrolls its playfield to the left draws
each new column of graphics into the column of memory that has just scrolled off the left edge,
just before it comes back in on the right. The picture never needs to be bigger than the screen.

| Layer | X offset | Y offset | The picture wraps at |
|---|---|---|---|
| ULA | NextReg `$26`: 0–255 | NextReg `$27`: 0–191 | 256 × 192 |
| LoRes | NextReg `$32`: 0–255 | NextReg `$33`: 0–191 | 256 × 192, in dots: one step is half a LoRes block |
| Layer 2 | NextRegs `$16` and `$71` bit 0: 0–319 (in 640 × 256, steps of two dots) | NextReg `$17`: 0–191 or 0–255 | its own size: 256 × 192, or 320 × 256 |
| Tilemap | NextRegs `$2F` and `$30`: 0–319, or 0–639 with 80 columns | NextReg `$31`: 0–255 | its own size: 320 or 640 × 256 |

The sprites have no scroll offset. They do not need one: every sprite has its own position, and
moving a sprite is a matter of changing it.

Because each layer has its own offsets, the layers can scroll at **different speeds**, which is how
a game makes a picture look deep: the far-away hills move slowly, the ground in front moves fast.
Here is the scene from the cast again, three frames apart:

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 224" role="img" aria-label="Three frames from Klive's Next with different scroll offsets. Left: no scroll. Middle: Layer 2 scrolled by 16 dots and the tilemap by 48: the hills have moved a little to the left, the platforms three times as far. Right: Layer 2 by 32 and the tilemap by 96: the platforms have moved further, and the left platform, run off the left edge, comes back in at the right edge. The score panel at the top and the sprites stay where they were." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>Scrolling on the real machine: Layer 2 and the tilemap scroll by their own offsets, so the platforms move three times as fast as the hills, the old trick of parallax. The ULA's panel and the sprites stay put. In the last frame the left platform has run off the left edge and comes back in on the right.</figcaption>
</figure>

Scrolling and clip windows work together. The clip window belongs to the screen, the scroll offset
to the layer's picture, so a layer can scroll inside a window that stays put, and a status panel
on another layer can stay exactly where it is. The ULA chapter's
[scrolling by the dot](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/ula/#scrolling-by-the-dot)
and the chapters on the other layers give the details of each layer's offsets; changing them in the
middle of a frame, for a screen that is split into bands that scroll separately, is the subject of the
raster effects chapter's
[split scroll](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/raster-effects/#a-status-bar-that-stands-still).

## Two answers for every dot

Here is the idea all the screen chapters are built on. For every position of the beam, every layer
gives the compositor exactly **two answers**:

1. **Which colour?** As an 8-bit **index** into a palette, not as a colour. The palette turns it
   into a colour later.
2. **Am I transparent here?** Whether this layer has anything to show at this dot at all.

That is all the compositor ever learns about a layer. It does not know about tiles, sprites,
attributes or bitmaps. Every rule the later chapters describe, from the Timex modes to sprite
anchors, is a way of arriving at these two answers. A few layers add a **flag** to them: the border
says "I am border", the tilemap says "I am behind the ULA", and Layer 2's palette can say
"draw me in front of everything".

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 370" role="img" aria-label="Four layer boxes on the left, each sending two answers to the compositor on the right: a colour index and whether it is transparent. ULA or LoRes: transparent if its colour equals the value in NextReg 14, if clipped, or if the ULA is switched off; flag: border. Tilemap: transparent if its dot equals the value in NextReg 4C, if clipped, or if the tilemap is off; flag: below the ULA. Layer 2: transparent if its colour equals the value in NextReg 14, if clipped, or if Layer 2 is off; flag: priority. Sprites: transparent if its dot equals the value in NextReg 4B, if clipped, where no sprite is, or if the sprites are off." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

</svg>
<figcaption>What each layer tells the compositor about one dot. The colour tests, against the value in NextReg <code>$14</code>, are made after the palette lookup; the dot tests, against the values in NextRegs <code>$4B</code> and <code>$4C</code>, before it.</figcaption>
</figure>

Look at the red lines in the figure: there are **two kinds of transparency**, and which a layer uses
says something about it.

- The ULA, LoRes and Layer 2 are transparent where their dot's **colour**, after the palette lookup,
  equals the **global transparency colour**, held in the Global Transparency Colour register, NextReg
  `$14`. These layers draw whole screens of colours, and "this colour means see-through" is the
  natural rule for a bitmap.
- The sprites and the tilemap are transparent where their **index**, before the lookup, equals the
  value in the Sprite Transparency Index register, NextReg `$4B` (sprites), or in the Tilemap
  Transparency Index register, NextReg `$4C` (tilemap). These layers draw shapes out of small 4- or
  8-bit patterns, and "this value in the pattern is the hole" is the natural rule for a shape.
  (The tilemap's text mode, whose tiles have one bit per dot, uses the colour rule instead.)

Both registers start at the same value, `$E3`, and so does the fallback colour. That is why `$E3`,
a bright magenta, is the colour of "nothing" on the Next, its way of saying "nobody home": in the
default Layer 2 palette, index `$E3` *is* colour `$E3`, so a Layer 2 bitmap filled with `$E3` is
invisible from the start.

A layer that is switched off, or clipped by its clip window at this dot, answers "transparent" too.
To the compositor there is no difference between "off", "outside my window" and "a hole in my
picture".

## The video pipeline

The compositor is not one step but a short **pipeline**, the video pipeline. A dot enters it when
the beam's counters reach its position and leaves it, as a colour, a few clock ticks later. While one
dot is in the last stage, the next ones are already in the stages before it, so a new dot comes out at
every tick. Here is the road from the beam's counters to the colours, in four stages:

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 408" role="img" aria-label="The video pipeline as four columns. Stage 0, at time 0, gathers the pixels: the ULA, LoRes, tilemap, Layer 2 and sprite modules each produce an index and their flags, and the registers that steer the rest are latched for this dot. Stage 1, one 7 MHz period later, looks the indices up in two palette memories of 1,024 entries each: one for the ULA and the tilemap, one for Layer 2 and the sprites, each read at 28 MHz, its two layers taking turns. Stage 2, half a period later, decides: transparency, the ULA and tilemap mixed into one layer U, the layer order from NextReg 15, the Layer 2 priority bit, the blend modes, and the fallback colour. Stage 3 only delays the result and adds the blanking; the colour leaves four 7 MHz periods after it was gathered." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>
  <defs>
    <marker id="ov6-arrow" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse">
      <path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="currentColor" fillOpacity="0.6" />
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <text x="60" y="18" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontWeight="600">stage 0: gather</text>

<figcaption>The video pipeline. Stage 1 has two boxes because the Next has two palette memories, read side by side; inside each, two layers take turns. Stage 3 changes nothing: it delays the colour by three more ticks, over which the logic of stage 2 can be spread so that it keeps up with the clock. The colour leaves four 7 MHz periods after it was gathered.</figcaption>
</figure>

Before we walk through the stages, though, there is one fact about what comes out of the end that
changes how you read everything else.

## Two dots for every position of the beam

So far, a dot has been one step of the beam's counters, one 7 MHz dot. The pipeline does not produce
one colour for it but **two**: a **left half** and a **right half**, each one 14 MHz dot wide, side by
side on the screen. Its last stages run fast enough for that: the decision (stage 2) is made at
14 MHz, and the palette lookups before it (stage 1) at 28 MHz, so both halves get their own.

For most layers the two halves are the same. A layer that draws 256 dots across, the ULA's standard
screen, LoRes, Layer 2 in 256 × 192 and 320 × 256, and every sprite, offers the same answers for both
halves, and the screen shows its dot twice as wide as a 14 MHz dot. The layers that draw 512 or 640
dots across, the ULA's Timex hi-res mode, the tilemap's 80 columns and Layer 2's 640 × 256, offer a
different answer for each half, and then the two halves really differ.

The compositor decides each half **on its own**, with that half's answers. So the layers can mix
inside one position of the beam: a hi-res tile dot that is opaque in the left half and transparent in
the right shows the tile in one half and whatever lies below it in the other.

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 284" role="img" aria-label="One position of the beam, one 7 MHz dot, drawn as its two halves, each a 14 MHz dot. The sprites offer one value for both halves: transparent. The tilemap in 80-column mode offers two values: an opaque tile dot in the left half and a transparent one in the right. The ULA's standard screen offers its paper colour in both halves. The compositor decides each half on its own, so the screen shows the tile in the left half and the ULA's paper in the right." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

</figure>

## Stage 0: gather

The beam's counters reach a position, and each producer offers its two answers for it. In the
same tick, the pipeline **latches the registers** that steer everything after it: the layer order,
the transparency and fallback colours, which palettes are shown, which layers are on. They are
held for this dot while it travels through the stages. That is how a write to one of these registers in
the middle of a line takes effect: from the next dot the beam reaches, and never half-way through one.
A few settings are taken less often, the ULA's scroll per 8-dot cell, the sprites a line ahead; the
raster effects chapter's
[when a write shows](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/raster-effects/#when-a-write-shows)
lists them, and that chapter is all about using this.

## Stage 1: look up

The indices go through the **palettes**. There are eight: a first and a second palette for each of
the ULA, the tilemap, Layer 2 and the sprites, 256 entries each. But there are only **two
memories**, each holding four of them: one for the ULA and the tilemap, one for Layer 2 and the
sprites. Two numbers describe each memory: **1,024 entries of 16 bits**.

**Why 1,024 entries?** Look at what picks an entry. Its address has ten bits. One says which of the
memory's two layers is looking up: the ULA or the tilemap in one memory, Layer 2 or the sprites in
the other. One says which of that layer's two palettes is shown, the first or the second (the Palette
Control register, NextReg `$43`, chooses for three of the layers, and the Tilemap Control register,
NextReg `$6B`, for the tilemap). The other eight are the layer's own index. Two layers, two palettes,
256 indices: 2 × 2 × 256 = 1,024, four palettes one after the other in one memory. (Entries, not
bytes: an entry is two bytes, so each memory holds 2K.)

**Why 16 bits?** An entry needs only ten: the 9-bit colour, and a **priority bit** written with it.
Only Layer 2's entries use that bit: it can lift a Layer 2 dot above everything, and it travels with
the colour from here on. Sixteen is simply what the memory is made of. One block of the FPGA's RAM
holds 16 Kbit, and laid out as 1,024 words each word is 16 bits wide: 1,024 × 16 is one whole block,
used as it comes, with six bits of every entry spare. Packed four to a block, the eight palettes take
two blocks of RAM; eight memories of their own would take far more of a chip that has few to spare.

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 294" role="img" aria-label="The layout of a palette memory. The address has 10 bits: bit 9 says which of the memory's two layers is looking up, bit 8 which of that layer's two palettes is shown, and bits 7 to 0 are the layer's own 8-bit index; that makes 1,024 entries. Each entry has 16 bits: bit 15 is the priority bit, used only by Layer 2; bits 14 to 9 are spare; bits 8 to 0 are the colour, three bits each of red, green and blue." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="395" y="286" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5" fillOpacity="0.8">1,024 entries × 16 bits = 16 Kbit: one block of the FPGA's RAM</text>
</svg>
<figcaption>What one palette memory holds. The address picks the layer, the palette and the index; the entry holds the colour and, for Layer 2, the priority bit. Ten of the sixteen bits carry meaning; the width is that of the block of RAM the memory is made of.</figcaption>
</figure>

Sharing has a price in time, and the pipeline pays it with speed. Each memory is read at **every
tick of the 28 MHz clock**, its two layers taking turns: the layer bit of the address says
which tick it is. That is two lookups for each 14 MHz dot, one for each layer, so all four layers
get their colours for every dot, even in the modes that draw 512 or 640 dots across.

This is why stage 1 is drawn as **two boxes** in the pipeline figure, not four. The two boxes are
not two steps one after the other: they are the two memories, and they work **at the same time**.
The taking of turns happens inside each box:

```text
28 MHz tick:       0         1         2         3      (one 7 MHz period)
memory 1:         ULA     tilemap     ULA     tilemap
memory 2:       Layer 2   sprites   Layer 2   sprites
```

Four boxes would show four memories, each with a lookup of its own. That is a natural design, but
not the Next's: its four lookups are made by two memories, two at a time. Neat, isn't it?

## Stage 2: decide

Now the compositor has four colours with their flags, and it applies the rules:

- **Is each layer transparent here?** The colour rule (against the value in the Global Transparency
  Colour register, NextReg `$14`) is checked now; the index rules were already applied by the sprites
  and the tilemap themselves.
- **The ULA and the tilemap become one layer, U.** Before the main order is applied, these two are
  mixed: the tilemap goes in front of the ULA, or behind it where a tile asks for it. In **stencil
  mode** a dot is shown only where both are opaque.
- **The order.** Three layers are left, **S** (sprites), **L** (Layer 2) and **U**, and the Sprite and
  Layers System register, NextReg `$15`, picks one of six orders, from SLU (sprites in front, the
  default) to ULS. The first opaque one, in that order, wins.
- **The exceptions.** A Layer 2 dot with the priority bit wins whatever the order. Two further
  settings of NextReg `$15` do not order Layer 2 and U at all but **add** their colours, channel by
  channel.
- **Nothing opaque?** Then the fallback colour, from the Fallback Colour register, NextReg `$4A`.

[The composition chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/composition/) follows
each of these rules in full, with the machine's own pictures.

## One dot through the pipeline

Let's take the dot marked in the exploded figure of the cast, in the default settings, and follow it.

<figcaption>The dot marked in the exploded figure, followed through the stages. Stage 0 gathers each layer's answer, stage 1 looks it up in the layer's palette, stage 2 decides. The ray stops at the first plane that is not transparent.</figcaption>
</figure>

Step by step:

1. **Stage 0.** The sprites have nothing at this position: transparent. Layer 2's bitmap holds
   `$E3` here, below its band of hills. The tilemap offers a dot of a platform tile, index `$13`.
   The ULA offers its paper colour.
2. **Stage 1.** Layer 2's `$E3` becomes the colour `$E3` (and a ninth bit). The tile's `$13`
   becomes whatever the tilemap palette holds at `$13`, a green.
3. **Stage 2.** Layer 2's colour equals NextReg `$14`: transparent. The tile is not behind the ULA,
   so U is the tile's green. The order is SLU: the sprites are transparent, Layer 2 is transparent, U
   is not. The dot is green.

Everything in the later chapters happens inside one of these three steps.

## Three clocks

Three numbers make it all fit:

- **28 MHz** is the master clock. Everything in the video pipeline counts in its ticks.
- **4 ticks per dot.** The beam's counters step at **7 MHz**, a quarter of the master clock: one
  step is one dot of a 256-wide line, the same dot the Spectrum's ULA sends at 7 MHz. Inside each
  step a sub-pixel counter counts the four master ticks, and the memories hand out those ticks in
  turn: the ULA and the tilemap take turns at bank 5, and each palette memory serves its two layers.
- **2 dots per step for the fine layers.** Layers that draw 512 or 640 dots across, the ULA's Timex
  hi-res mode, the tilemap's 80 columns and Layer 2's 640 mode, run at **14 MHz**, two dots per 7
  MHz step. Stage 2 decides at 14 MHz too, so the final picture has 14 MHz resolution across, and a
  256-wide layer offers each of its dots twice.

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 352" role="img" aria-label="The clock tree. A 28 MHz master clock, 28.0 MHz in VGA mode 0 and on HDMI and up to 33 MHz in VGA mode 6, gives three clocks for the picture. At 28 MHz itself: the sub-pixel counter, four slots per dot, used for turns at the memories and the palette lookups. At 14 MHz: the ULA's dots, the tilemap, Layer 2's 640 mode and stage 2. At 7 MHz: the beam's counters, stage 0, the sprites, LoRes and Layer 2's 256 and 320 modes. The CPU clock is chosen from the same clocks by NextReg 07: 3.5 MHz made from the 7 MHz clock, or 7, 14 or 28 MHz." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  {/* prose-ok: the figure lists the four CPU speeds */}
  <text x="350" y="336" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">3.5 MHz (from 7 MHz, held back for contention) · 7 · 14 · 28 MHz</text>
  <line x1="113" y1="240" x2="113" y2="288" stroke="currentColor" strokeOpacity="0.4" strokeDasharray="3 3" markerEnd="url(#ov7-arrow)" />
  <line x1="350" y1="240" x2="350" y2="288" stroke="currentColor" strokeOpacity="0.4" strokeDasharray="3 3" markerEnd="url(#ov7-arrow)" />
  <line x1="587" y1="240" x2="587" y2="288" stroke="currentColor" strokeOpacity="0.4" strokeDasharray="3 3" markerEnd="url(#ov7-arrow)" />
</svg>
<figcaption>Which part of the picture runs on which clock. The CPU's clock is not a fourth clock but one of the same three, or the 7 MHz clock with every other tick taken out to make 3.5 MHz.</figcaption>
</figure>

The VGA modes 1 to 6 (the Video Timing register, NextReg `$11`) raise the master clock in steps up
to 33 MHz, for monitors that do not accept the standard rate. Everything above scales with it: the
frame keeps its shape and only gets faster.

The CPU deserves a sentence. At the top level of the design, the CPU's clock is **selected from the
video clocks**: 28, 14 or 7 MHz as they are, or 3.5 MHz made from the 7 MHz clock by letting only
every other tick through. Contention, at 3.5 MHz, is that clock holding back a tick. So the CPU
speed changes how much a program can do in a frame, never the picture: the beam's counters run at
7 MHz whatever the CPU does.

## 512 colours

Every path through the pipeline ends in the same form: a **9-bit colour**, 3 bits each of red,
green and blue, **RGB333**, 512 colours. Why 9? Three bits a channel is what the Next's video
connector puts out: a 3-bit digital-to-analogue converter per channel for VGA and RGB. HDMI carries
8 bits a channel, and the 3 bits are stretched to fill them.

<figure>
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 170" role="img" aria-label="All 512 colours of RGB333, drawn as eight slices of 64 colours, one slice for each of the eight blue levels 0 to 7. </svg>
<figcaption>The Next's 512 colours, one square each, as eight slices by blue level. The ringed squares are the ZX Spectrum's 15 colours as the Next's default ULA palette makes them: each channel off, at level 5, or at level 7 with BRIGHT. Bright magenta is the one exception, a step of green away from its corner.</figcaption>
</figure>

Fifteen, not sixteen: the Spectrum has 16 colour codes, eight colours with and without BRIGHT, but
BRIGHT black is the same black. And one of the fifteen has moved. Pure bright magenta, red 7, green 0,
blue 7, is `$E3` in the 8-bit form, the global transparency colour from Two answers for every dot: on
the ULA layer every bright magenta dot would be a hole. So the Next's default palette gives bright
magenta one step of green, a shade the eye hardly tells apart and the compositor never mistakes for
"nothing". Sneaky, and rather clever.

The palettes are what connect the 8-bit indices of the two answers to these 9-bit colours, and the
8-bit indices are what keep the layers cheap: a Layer 2 dot is one byte, not two. An 8-bit palette
entry can also be written in the short form `RRRGGGBB`, which leaves out the lowest bit of blue; the
[palettes chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/palettes/) explains how that bit
is made, and where the eight palettes are selected and written.

## What stays the same

All this is added *beside* the Spectrum, not on top of it. Switch the Next to 48K or 128K timing and
leave the new layers off, as an old program does, and what is left is the Spectrum:

- **The frame** keeps the Spectrum's shape: 448 dots by 312 lines for the 48K, 456 by 311 for the
  128K and +3 (the frame drawn in Where each layer can draw), and 448 by 320 for a Pentagon. A dot is
  still half a T-state of a 3.5 MHz CPU, so a line still lasts 224 or 228 T-states.
- **The ULA reads the screen the same way**: a bitmap byte and an attribute for every 8 dots, in
  the same order and at the same moments relative to the interrupt.
- **Contention** stops the CPU at the same T-states, in the same pattern. It exists only while the
  CPU runs at **3.5 MHz**: at 7, 14 or 28 MHz the CPU is never held back (and bit 6 of the Peripheral 3
  Setting register, NextReg `$08`, switches it off at 3.5 MHz too). That is the price of speed being
  paid by compatibility: a program that counts T-states against the beam works only at the speed it
  was written for.
- **The border** is still port `$FE`, changed at the same moments.

The Spectrum screen chapter's explanation of all this, from the frame to the floating bus, holds for
the Next's ULA. What the Next changes about the ULA is the subject of the
[ULA chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/ula/).

## The screen chapters

So, what comes next? A chapter for each plane of the stack, and a few about the picture as a whole:

<figure>

<figcaption>The screen chapters as a map: a chapter for each plane of the stack (the ULA's plane has two, the ULA and LoRes), and five about the picture as a whole, this one among them.</figcaption>
</figure>

Two chapters lay the ground, and I would read them before all the others. The
[frame chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/timing/) covers the beam's
counters, the timing modes, 50 and 60 Hz, the interrupts, and the road from the counters to VGA and
HDMI. The [palettes chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/palettes/) covers
RGB333, the eight palettes and how they are written, both kinds of transparency, and the fallback
colour.

Then come the layers, one chapter each, in any order you like.
[The ULA chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/ula/) extends the Spectrum's
screen with the Timex modes, ULANext, ULA+, scrolling and clipping.
[The LoRes chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/lores/) explains LoRes and
Radastan, the 128 × 96 modes that take the ULA's place.
[The Layer 2 chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/layer2/) explains its three
resolutions, and why two of them store their bytes down the screen.
[The tilemap chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/tilemap/) explains maps,
tiles, mirroring and rotation, in front of the ULA or behind it. And
[the sprites chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/sprites/) explains their
attributes, anchors, the line buffer and its time budget.

After the layer chapters,
[the composition chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/composition/) puts them
together: stage 2 in full, with the six orders, stencils and blending. Last comes
[the raster effects chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/raster-effects/), on
changing the picture mid-frame: the Copper, line interrupts, and how Klive keeps up with the beam. It
comes last because it changes the settings of all the others while a frame is drawn.

## How Klive does it

Klive's Next core is a C program compiled to WebAssembly, written to behave exactly like the
hardware (see
[The ZX Spectrum Next Core and the FPGA](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-core/)). It
draws the picture in two steps that mirror the pipeline, but grouped differently: not dot by dot
through every stage, but layer by layer through stages 0 and 1, and then dot by dot through
stage 2.

The first step works in **one buffer per layer**. The picture Klive shows is the **360 × 288** window
from Where each layer can draw, stored as 720 × 288 pixels: one pixel for every 14 MHz dot, so a 7 MHz
dot is two pixels. Each of the four layers (LoRes draws into the ULA's) has a buffer of its own of the
same size, with **16 bits per pixel**:

```text
  bit:  15        14–12     11       10        9          8–0
       OPAQUE     –       BORDER  TM_BELOW  L2_PRIORITY   9-bit RGB
```

A layer's renderer does stages 0 and 1 for the whole of its buffer: it works out its dot, looks up
the palette, applies its transparency rule (the colour against the value in the Global Transparency
Colour register, NextReg `$14`, for the ULA and Layer 2, the index for the tilemap and the sprites),
and writes either 0, for transparent, or `OPAQUE`, the colour and its flags. These are the two answers
and their flags, with the palette lookup already done. The border is drawn into the ULA's buffer
first, as ULA pixels marked `BORDER`, and the ULA's picture over it.

The second step is **one function for stage 2**. `zxnextUlaCompose` goes through the picture dot by
dot and calls `zxnextComposePixel` with the four values of that dot. That function is the hardware's
stage 2 written in C, rule for rule: the ULA and tilemap mix, stencil mode, the six orders with the
border exception, the priority bit, both blend modes and the fallback. It also returns, if asked,
*which rule* chose the colour; that is what the IDE's **Layers** view and its pixel probe show when
you point at a dot (see [Layers](/en/guides/next/layers/)).

Most programs never switch on a layer beyond the ULA, so the compose has a **fast path**: when the
tilemap, Layer 2 and the sprites are all off and no blend mode is set, every dot is the ULA's
colour or the fallback, and the general function is skipped.

There is one catch. The renderers draw from the registers and memory *as they are now*. If they ran
once per frame, a palette change at line 100 would recolour the whole frame. So, as Klive's Spectrum
cores do, the Next core **follows the beam** and draws lazily: just before a write that could change
the picture, a Copper move, a palette write, a border `OUT`, it renders the lines the beam has already
passed with the old state. The raster effects chapter's
[How Klive does it](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/raster-effects/#how-klive-does-it)
explains this raster and the one line of difference it allows. And if you want to read the code
yourself, here is where it lives:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The layer buffers, the renderers, `zxnextComposePixel` and `zxnextUlaCompose` | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-ula.c` |
| Layer 2, tilemap and sprite state (registers, banks, attributes) | `zxnext-layer2.c`, `zxnext-tilemap.c`, `zxnext-sprites.c` in the same folder |
| The eight palettes and their default contents | `zxnext-palette.c` |
| The frame sizes for each timing mode | `zxnext.c`, `zxnext-nextreg.c` |

## What to take home

So, what have we got? The Next keeps the Spectrum's **ULA**, bit-compatible, and adds four producers
beside it: **LoRes**, **Layer 2**, the **tilemap** and the **sprites**. Each reads its own memory, and
all of them run at once. Five producers make **four layers**, because the ULA and LoRes share one:
LoRes, when it is on, replaces the ULA's picture, and the border stays the ULA's either way.

The ULA draws the 256 × 192 display area and the **border** around it; Layer 2's wide modes, the
tilemap and the sprites can draw over the **wide area**, 320 × 256. A clip window fences each layer
in, and scroll offsets move its picture without moving a byte. Where no layer has a dot, the
**fallback colour** from NextReg `$4A` shows.

For every dot, every layer gives two answers, **a colour index** and **"am I transparent?"**,
sometimes with a flag, and the compositor sees nothing else. Transparency comes in two kinds: by
**colour** after the palette (NextReg `$14`, for the ULA, LoRes and Layer 2) and by **index** before
it (NextReg `$4B` for the sprites, NextReg `$4C` for the tilemap). All three, and the fallback, start
at `$E3`.

The **pipeline** gathers the answers and latches the registers (stage 0), looks the indices up in
eight palettes held in two time-shared memories of 1,024 entries of 16 bits (stage 1), and decides the
winner (stage 2): the ULA and tilemap mixed into one layer, then one of six orders, the priority bit,
blending, the fallback. It runs on a **28 MHz** master clock; the beam steps at 7 MHz, the fine layers
and the decision at 14 MHz, so every 7 MHz dot comes out as two. Every colour ends as **RGB333**, 512
colours, and the CPU's clock is picked from the same clocks and never changes the picture.

Klive renders each layer into a **16-bit buffer** (stages 0 and 1) and composes them dot by dot with a
C copy of **stage 2**, lazily, in step with the beam. That is the whole map. The chapters that follow
zoom in on one corner of it at a time.20:I[8500,["545","$16","500","$17","436","$18","25","$19"],""]

Each is a separate piece of\nhardware with its own memory and its own idea of what a picture is: a bitmap with a byte per dot, a\ngrid of tiles, a set of sprites. A ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"compositor"}]," after them\npicks the winner."]}]

28:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Look at the memories on the left. The producers do not queue for one memory, as the CPU and the\nULA do on the Spectrum. The FPGA keeps ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"bank 5"}]," (and the first 8K of bank 7, the 128K’s second\nscreen) in its own block RAM, which has two ports: one is shared by the CPU and LoRes, the other by\nthe ULA and the tilemap. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Layer 2"}]," reads the external SRAM, the Next’s main memory, where its\nbitmap can be large. The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"sprites"}]," keep their shapes in a 16K memory of their own, which the CPU\ncan reach only through I/O ports. It is block RAM inside the FPGA, and a very fast one: the sprite\nhardware can read a new byte from it at ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"every tick of the 28 MHz master clock"}],", four times for\nevery dot the beam draws. That speed is what lets it draw dozens of sprites into a line in the time\nthe beam takes to draw one (the sprites chapter’s\n",["$","$L20",null,{"href":"/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/sprites/#the-time-budget-of-a-line","prefetch":"$undefined","className":"x:focus-visible:nextra-focus x:text-primary-600 x:underline x:hover:no-underline x:decoration-from-font x:[text-underline-position:from-font]","children":"time budget"}]," shows\nhow). Giving each producer its own way to its data is what lets them all run at once."]}]

2c:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The layers are a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"stack"}],". 2d:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The order of the stack is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"not fixed"}],". The Sprite and Layers System register, NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$15"}],", picks\none of six orders for the sprites, Layer 2 and the ULA (with the tilemap), so a program can put Layer 2\nin front of the sprites, or the Spectrum screen in front of everything. 2e:["$","ul",null,{"className":"x:[:is(ol,ul)_&]:my-[.75em] x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:list-disc x:ms-[1.5em]","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The ULA."}]," The Spectrum’s own screen, ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$4000"}]," in bank 5 or the shadow screen in bank 7, with\nall of its rules. 38:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["A layer does not have to cover the whole of its canvas. Each layer has a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"clip window"}],", a\nrectangle on the screen. Inside it, the layer draws as usual. Outside it, the layer answers\n“transparent” for every dot, so the dot comes from the layers below it, exactly as if the layer had\nnothing to show there (Two answers for every dot, further on, explains the answer)."]}]
39:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Notice what a clip window is ",["$","em",null,{"children":"not"}],". It does not change the layer’s memory, and it does not move or\nshrink the layer’s picture: it is a mask laid over the screen, which hides part of the picture and\nshows what is underneath. That makes it the cheapest tool on the machine for a whole family of\neffects: a score panel that one layer must not draw over, a playfield framed by a border, a layer\nswitched off for a band of the screen, a letterbox. One write of four bytes does it, where clearing\nthe same area in memory would take thousands of bytes, and putting it back would take thousands\nmore. I would call that a bargain."]}]

3c:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Each window is four bytes, ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"X1, X2, Y1, Y2"}],", and both edges are inside it: a window of X 0–255 is\n256 dots wide. 3e:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The right half of the figure explains the odd numbers. A coordinate is one byte, 0 to 255, but the\nwide area is 320 dots across. 43:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Moving a picture on the Spectrum meant moving bytes. To scroll the screen one character to the left,\na program had to copy almost all of the 6,912 bytes of the screen."}]
44:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Scrolling by ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"one dot"}]," is harder still. Eight dots share a byte, so no byte can simply be copied:\nevery bitmap byte has to be shifted by one bit, and the bit that falls out of it carried into its\nneighbour, along every line, in the screen’s scrambled address order. Even with the tightest code,\nan unrolled ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"RL (HL)"}]," and ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"DEC L"}]," for each byte, that is 19 T-states for each of the 6,144 bytes:\nabout 117,000 T-states, more than one and a half frames of a 48K, before the contention of the screen\nmemory slows it down further. And that moves only the dots. The colours belong to 8 × 8 cells and\ncan move only a whole character at a time, so the picture tears into ink that has moved and colours\nthat have not. That is why Spectrum games that scroll smoothly scroll a small window, not the whole\nscreen."]}]
45:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Bigger pictures make it worse: to move a 48K Layer 2 picture by copying, a 3.5 MHz CPU would need\nabout fourteen frames. All of this is far too slow for anything that has to move every frame."}]
46:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["So the Next lets the hardware do it. All the producers except the sprites have ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"scroll offsets"}],":\nregisters that say where in its picture the layer starts reading. With an offset of zero, the dot at\nthe top-left of the layer’s area is the first dot of its picture. With an X offset of 96, it is the\n97th dot of the first line, and everything shifts 96 dots to the left. One register write moves the\nwhole picture."]}]

5a:["$","figure",null,{"children":[["$","svg",null,{"viewBox":"0 0 700 408","role":"img","aria-label":"The video pipeline as four columns. Stage 0, at time 0, gathers the pixels: the ULA, LoRes, tilemap, Layer 2 and sprite modules each produce an index and their flags, and the registers that steer the rest are latched for this dot. 62:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The beam’s counters reach a position, and each producer offers its two answers for it. In the\nsame tick, the pipeline ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"latches the registers"}]," that steer everything after it: the layer order,\nthe transparency and fallback colours, which palettes are shown, which layers are on. They are\nheld for this dot while it travels through the stages. That is how a write to one of these registers in\nthe middle of a line takes effect: from the next dot the beam reaches, and never half-way through one.\nA few settings are taken less often, the ULA’s scroll per 8-dot cell, the sprites a line ahead; the\nraster effects chapter’s\n",["$","$L20",null,{"href":"/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-display/raster-effects/#when-a-write-shows","prefetch":"$undefined","className":"x:focus-visible:nextra-focus x:text-primary-600 x:underline x:hover:no-underline x:decoration-from-font x:[text-underline-position:from-font]","children":"when a write shows"}],"\nlists them, and that chapter is all about using this."]}]

64:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The indices go through the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"palettes"}],". There are eight: a first and a second palette for each of\nthe ULA, the tilemap, Layer 2 and the sprites, 256 entries each. But there are only ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"two\nmemories"}],", each holding four of them: one for the ULA and the tilemap, one for Layer 2 and the\nsprites. Two numbers describe each memory: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"1,024 entries of 16 bits"}],"."]}]
65:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Why 1,024 entries?"}]," Look at what picks an entry. Its address has ten bits. One says which of the\nmemory’s two layers is looking up: the ULA or the tilemap in one memory, Layer 2 or the sprites in\nthe other. One says which of that layer’s two palettes is shown, the first or the second (the Palette\nControl register, NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$43"}],", chooses for three of the layers, and the Tilemap Control register,\nNextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$6B"}],", for the tilemap). The other eight are the layer’s own index. Two layers, two palettes,\n256 indices: 2 × 2 × 256 = 1,024, four palettes one after the other in one memory. (Entries, not\nbytes: an entry is two bytes, so each memory holds 2K.)"]}]
66:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Why 16 bits?"}]," An entry needs only ten: the 9-bit colour, and a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"priority bit"}]," written with it.\nOnly Layer 2’s entries use that bit: it can lift a Layer 2 dot above everything, and it travels with\nthe colour from here on. Sixteen is simply what the memory is made of. One block of the FPGA’s RAM\nholds 16 Kbit, and laid out as 1,024 words each word is 16 bits wide: 1,024 × 16 is one whole block,\nused as it comes, with six bits of every entry spare. Packed four to a block, the eight palettes take\ntwo blocks of RAM; eight memories of their own would take far more of a chip that has few to spare."]}]
67:["$","figure",null,{"children":[["$","svg",null,{"viewBox":"0 0 700 294","role":"img","aria-label":"The layout of a palette memory. The address has 10 bits: bit 9 says which of the memory's two layers is looking up, bit 8 which of that layer's two palettes is shown, and bits 7 to 0 are the layer's own 8-bit index; that makes 1,024 entries. 98:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"pipeline"}]," gathers the answers and latches the registers (stage 0), looks the indices up in\neight palettes held in two time-shared memories of 1,024 entries of 16 bits (stage 1), and decides the\nwinner (stage 2): the ULA and tilemap mixed into one layer, then one of six orders, the priority bit,\nblending, the fallback.
